@@26233861 <p> For a few days this week , Australians seriously reckoned with the idea of Peter Dutton as prime minister . <p> Just three more party room votes and he would have been in the Lodge . <p> Opinion polls show he may not be popular with the public , but that does not mean the issues he champions are not resonating . <p> He built his campaign around what he and others have identified as a change in Australian politics ; it is a shift that aligns with the ideological battles that have so profoundly reshaped global politics . <p> Internationally , people are reclaiming the idea of national identity ; there is a blowback against globalisation ; a rejection of political elites and politics-as-usual ; immigration , free trade , energy policy have become defining issues . <p> Politics is becoming increasingly polarised and fractured . <p> It is disrupting democracies , redefining ideological boundaries ; trade barriers have gone back up , borders have been strengthened . <p> British journalist and political observer David Goodhart has written that the two events have marked , " not so much the arrival of the populist era but its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ see populism as the enemy of democracy , but in many ways its essence . <p> Those left behind are now being heard after too long being ignored by the so-called left progressives , and their belief in open borders and free trade . <p> Hillary Clinton called them " the deplorables " ; this is the deplorables ' revenge . <p> Goodhart says we are seeing a " core values divide " . <p> As Goodhart puts it : " We are in a border war between nationalists , mainly on the right , and multicultural globalists mainly on the left . " <p> This border war has reignited old ideological battles . In his new book , Conservatism : An Invitation to the Great Tradition , philosopher Roger Scruton writes : <p> " Issues that were for years undiscussable have appeared at the top of the agenda , and demographic and strategic changes have redrawn the map of the world . <p> " We should not be surprised if people previously unimaginable as the holders of high office suddenly emerge at the top . " <p> Mr Scruton @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about their beliefs and political goals . <h> The civil war goes on <p> This is the environment Peter Dutton and his supporters have sought to capitalise on . <p> Questions of climate change , energy policy , immigration , freedom of speech and religion have been weaponised in an ideological battle to win back the soul of the Liberal Party , to reclaim it for its conservative base . <p> Mr Dutton was seen as a leader who could drag back the disaffected Liberal voters who have defected to Pauline Hanson 's One Nation . <p> Outgoing prime minister Malcolm Turnbull called this an insurgency . <p> Scott Morrison 's triumph though only reveals the extent of this Liberal Party civil war : we know where the battle lines are drawn and where the troops are positioned . <h> Australia is a shining light <p> Can Australia withstand the tide of political history that has swamped much of Europe and the United States ? <p> Overall , our democracy is strong : the Economist Intelligence Unit 's Global Democracy Index has placed Australia in the top 10 for the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lights in a pall of gloom . Compulsory voting is a critical factor in our favour ; it inoculates us against some of the voter apathy or extremism of other countries . <p> Despite recent concerns about integration and immigration levels , we do n't have the deep concentrated ethnic divides of the United States or Europe . <p> We escaped the worst of the global financial crisis and we are far from the conflict hotspots of the world . Our social safety net offers support to our most vulnerable and disadvantaged . <p> But , there are worrying signs . The public is increasingly fed up with politicians , especially the two major parties . At the 2016 federal election , up to a third of voters rejected the ALP or the Liberal-National coalition . <p> Opinion polls indicate nothing has changed . Australians know the power of their vote and are prepared to brutally wield it . At state and federal levels there is increased volatility rewarding more minor parties and independents . Voters have shown themselves especially keen on shaking up the Senate . <h> Inequality increasing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ between rich and poor . <p> According to the International Monetary Fund , we are among the nations with the fastest growing rates of income inequality . <p> Last year , the OECD economic survey of Australia found " inclusiveness has been eroded " . The earnings of the top households grew by more than 40 per cent between 2004 and 2014 : the lowest by 25 per cent . <p> Around the world , this has proved fertile ground for political extremism , and the worst of populism . <p> We are also becoming more distrustful and angry . The Edelman research and PR group 's 2017 Trust Barometer showed Australia was among those countries losing faith in institutions . <p> Peter Dutton 's leadership ambitions have been thwarted , but the issues that have fired his campaign remain . <p> Australians are not easily given to extremism , pollsters constantly remind us we are a nation of centrists . <p> But we are not immune to the winds of politics blowing through our world .

" and the project lead says what they found so far could give us a new perspective on what life was like in the 18th century . <p> Experts say there could be as many as a thousand bodies buried at Rochefort Point , the main burial site at Cape Breton 's Fortress of Louisbourg , once a popular seaport and the site @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the 1700s . <p> The narrow peninsula extending into the ocean just beyond the fortress 's east gate is now under a siege of its own : rising sea levels and coastal erosion pose a distant threat to the centuries of history buried beneath its surface , said Parks Canada spokesman David Ebert . <p> " As we face climate change and we have bigger storms that have gotten more energy , that can accelerate the erosion problem , " he said . <p> " But we think that doing this type of work before it 's an imminent threat , while it 's still a lower level threat , we can do this in a respectful manner . " <p> According to historical maps , it 's estimated Rochefort Point has shrunk to only about half of its original size thanks to coastal erosion , Ebert said . <p> Students from across North America have unearthed the remains of 31 people so far this year . ( The Canadian Press/Parks Canada ) <p> Parks Canada , which owns and operates the partially reconstructed Fortress of Louisbourg , teamed up @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a yearly summer program for students across the continent to help excavate and study the remains . <p> Once the remains are analyzed , they will be moved and reburied at another burial site . <p> " It 's important to treat these remains with the dignity and respect they deserve , " Ebert said . <p> University of New Brunswick bioarcheologist Amy Scott is the project lead for the excavation group , which consists of 15 students from different universities throughout Canada and the United States . <p> Most are from bioanthropology programs , but Scott said their degrees span a number of anthropological and archeological fields . <h> Average age of death 24 <p> This year , the students unearthed the remains of 31 people , and Scott said most of the remains were male , as men ? ? " many of them soldiers ? ? " greatly outnumbered women at the site in the 1700s . The average age of death was 24 . <p> Last year , the group only found adult remains , but this year they uncovered six child burials , which suggests @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ graveyard . <p> Scott explained the French primarily used the graveyard for their soldiers , but the New Englanders used it for both soldiers and civilians ? ? " including children . <h> ' Life was tough at Louisbourg ' <p> She added that child mortality in those times was quite high . <p> " Life was tough at Louisbourg for the adults , and even more so for the children , " she said . <p> Many of the remains show signs of blunt force trauma and fractures on their faces and hands , suggesting quite a bit of brawling went on at the fortress during the tumultuous time in Canadian history . <p> Scott said unearthing and studying these remains can help us make connections to a time period that seems almost alien in the 21st century . <p> " Sometimes there 's this misconception that people in the past were vastly different than what we are today in terms of their daily experiences . ( The Canadian Press/Parks Canada ) <p> " But at the end of the day , we 're all still just people . " <p> During analysis , she 's found bodies with signs of fractured limbs and cavities in their teeth : afflictions that people in the present day can relate to . <p> Seeing these connections with her own eyes has been a humbling experience for Scott , who said wanting to know who we are and where we came from is a near-universal question . <h> Overcoming the odds <p> While the main goal of the project is to rescue the bodies from the vulnerable peninsula and give them a proper burial , she said the experience of analyzing the remains has been a reminder of the resilience and strength of mankind . <p> " We think about how challenging life would 've been for these folks , and the fact that they were @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lot of medical intervention in most cases makes us think about what our lives are like , and how we 've benefitted from these medical interventions that can help us , " she said . <p> Haylee Meloche , who is going into her fifth year of forensic science and criminology at the University of Windsor in Ontario , said the on-site experience and historical aspect is what drew her to the project . <p> While experts are hesitant to predict how long Rochefort Point has left before it 's swallowed by the sea , Meloche stressed that time is of the essence . <h> ' It just ca n't be lost ' <p> " Preserving the history , especially in a place like Louisburg where there is such rich history , losing that all to the coast would be such a loss to our heritage as people here , " she said . <p> " Even though I 'm not from this area , just the fact that there is a set of history that we can still discover , it just ca n't be lost . Thirty years ago , harvests in October were not unheard of . <p> According to Toubart and the Champagne winegrowers ' union , the more than 15,000 Champagne winegrowers will likely harvest between 13,000 and 16,000 kilograms of grapes per hectare . <p> That leaves a comfortable margin for producers to use only the best grapes and rebuild reserves used up in recent years since the industry has agreed this year to bring 10,800 kilograms per hectare to market . <p> " We have never experienced this before in the Champagne region , in terms of quantity and quality , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' union . <p> " This will enable us to put into cellar amazing wines which will be on the market in three years , " he added . <p> Each year , 310 million bottles of French Champagne - whose geographic designation is strictly controlled as a brand - are sold worldwide while more than a billion are stored in cellars waiting for the right moment to be savoured.

The first is a cosmopolitan nation in the big cities filled with people of all races and creeds and what British author David Goodhart calls " anywheres " : prosperous world citizens who have global opportunities . Then there is outer suburban Australia and the bush , full of so-called " somewheres " , rooted in , and committed to the place and traditions in which they find themselves . In this new cultural faultline , climate change has become a proxy for the conservative culture wars . @@26272535 <p> The boomers have vacated the prime minister 's chair . Scott Morrison was born in 1968 , the year the younger boomers came of age , and really started causing trouble . He is the first Gen Xer in the top job . <p> His deputy , Josh Frydenberg , is a child of 1971 and so also a member of the same neglected generational grouping , born from the mid-1960s to the early ' 80s . <p> Our new leaders are making sure we know they are a little younger . The central message of the LNP since the toppling of Malcolm Turnbull is that a " new generation " is taking over . <p> Will Gen Xers rally around their new Gen X Prime Minister ? Not a chance ? ? " and it 's not because Gen Xers are too cynical to rally around anyone . <p> We tend to use the concept of generations simplistically , primarily to further bad stereotypes about other age cohorts ? ? " " narcissistic millennials " , " selfish baby boomers " , " cynical Gen Xers " . <p> There is , at best , just a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ shifts between cohorts in average attitudes towards work , family and political questions , these are often small . <p> They get warped into a narrative of generational defining oppositions , when we are divided and united in many other ways that tend to be more salient for our politics . <h> Generations share a sense the stakes have changed <p> There is another way to think about a generation : as a group who create a new way of doing things when the old ways do n't work anymore . <p> Crisis events can create conditions in which those building a life afterwards can not , or will not , live their lives like the generation before them . <p> This way of thinking about generations was developed by scholars grappling with the consequences of World War I and the " lost generation " left in its wake . <p> Politically and socially , people within a generation can be in violent opposition . The new movements built by the young people who lived through World War I set the scene for the next great conflict . <p> What @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ set of attributes but that they share a sense that the stakes have changed , and we can not go on like before . <p> Political scientists use this framework to highlight that the boomers , for example , came of age alongside the rise of the " new right " as well as the " new left " and should not be too closely aligned with the counterculture and resistance to the Vietnam War . <p> They ushered in a shifting of the political stakes towards culture , just as the rise of precarious work , climate change and social media are again shifting politics across the spectrum of positions . <p> While not a crisis on the scale of armed conflict , federal politics over the past decade has left numerous vanquished boomer prime ministers in its wake , and an increasing frustrated citizenry . <p> The exasperation is palpable ; we can not go on like this . This is what Scott Morrison and the Liberal Party are hoping to tap into by saying that they represent the " next " and " new " generation ; @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Morrison is no doubt hoping , likely against the odds , to hold on to the top job for a while . <p> Whatever the longevity of this government , we are going to have at least a couple of Gen Xers in the top job over the coming decade , before those pesky millennials come for it . <p> But appointing a leader born a few years later than the last few does not make a new generation , at least not in a very meaningful sense . <p> It will be meaningful to talk of a new generation when the issues shift , or the way politics is done shifts . <h> Our politics has strained the generational conflict <p> Mr Morrison and Mr Frydenberg 's pitch is also that this new generation will govern for all of us . This is telling in the context of a generational politics increasingly portrayed through the avocado-tinged lens of generational conflict . <p> It is only a team of Generation Xers who could get away with a pitch like this . <p> Up until now , Gen X has been @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Left to deal with the bills and family challenges that tend to come with middle age and , if there 's time , to argue among themselves over which is the best of Ethan Hawke 's films . <p> While boomers getting rich at the expense of millennials is too simplistic a narrative , our politics has strained the generational contract at a collective level , pushing people back on the resources of their family , whether they are young , old or in-between . <p> Will we will look back at this coming generation of leaders as the ones who discovered how to tame the pressures of contemporary politics , where civilisational challenges are paired with creeping everyday insecurities in the context of an ever accelerating and often toxic media and political culture ? Maybe , but the odds are long . <p> Maybe , and hopefully , this generation of leaders can set the stage for renewing the generational contract in Australia . <p> This would be governing for all of us , across generations and other divides , in a meaningful way , even as deep @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) remain . <p> Dan Woodman is TR Ashworth Associate Professor of Sociology in the School of Social and Political Sciences at the University of Melbourne .

@@26290046 <h> Australia thrust back into energy policy wilderness <p> LAST week 's tumultuous events in Canberra unfortunately spell the end to hopes of there being , any time soon at least , a coherent national policy that addresses both climate and energy objectives in Australia . <p> Australians , from within communities to the big end of town , overwhelmingly want our parliament to deliver on this . <p> Indeed , the most recent Lowy Institute poll found nine out of 10 Australians want our government to take steps to tackle climate change , two-thirds of those saying we should do so even if it involves significant costs . But once again , the issue has somehow undone a government . <p> It is now completely unclear what sort of energy policy , if any , the current Australian Government might bring to parliament . <p> The energy sector must therefore continue to sit and wait for much-needed investment certainty . <p> Let 's recap on why a coherent energy policy in Australia is needed . <p> Australia is a signatory , for now at least , to the Paris Climate Agreement , designed to limit global temperature rise @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has committed to reduce our emissions from all sources ( not just the electricity sector ) to 26-28 per cent below 2005 levels by 2030 . <p> Forecasting emissions is difficult , not least of all because the uptake of low emissions technologies is heavily influenced both by changing technology costs and by changing government policy . <p> However , according to the Government 's own figures and most recent projections , we 're on target for 2030 emissions that will be only about 7 per cent below 2005 levels . <p> Put simply , under a business-as-usual scenario we 're likely to be a fair way short of our commitment . <p> The good news is that a number of key measures , mandating energy efficiency and increasing the amount of renewable energy generation via the Renewable Energy Target for example , have been quite successful at improving our emissions trajectory . <p> But , we need to do much more . We need appropriate , forward-looking policy settings that will enable the investments required to facilitate a well-planned transition to an appropriate mix of technologies . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ economy-wide emissions , is the best equipped to deliver significant emissions reductions . <p> In fact , the electricity sector could and should be shouldering more of the emissions reductions in the short to medium term . It will be cheaper , easier and faster to transition our electricity system to low emissions than it will be to transition , for example , the transport or agriculture sectors . <p> Solar and wind are now generally the cheapest forms of new-build electricity generation available , in most locations around the world . <p> Increasing the level of renewable generation has been shown ( by experience in Germany for example , and via the NEG modelling in Australia ) to actually drive energy prices down rather than up . <p> Reliability issues that may arise in a high renewables future can be addressed through new generation and balancing technologies , such as pumped hydro energy storage or battery storage . <p> It will take some planning and some work , but it can be done . <p> A well thought through and coherent policy could address all three of these energy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> In the period leading up to last week , I noticed a concerning shift in the political commentary on energy policy , towards one where " lower household energy bills " has almost become the only game in town . It is of course hard to argue against such a motive on its own , yet to focus on this at expense of all else is problematic . <p> It takes focus away from the broader energy system issues and it effectively locks out solutions that might actually address all issues simultaneously . Interestingly too , no-one yet seems to be asking the alternative question : " What will be the impact on future household energy bills if we do n't act to reduce emissions ? " <p> Higher electricity demand and prices are usually associated with hotter days , while extreme weather events often translate to electricity price spikes . <p> Evan Franklin is an associate professor in the Centre for Renewable Energy and Power Systems and co-director of the Future Energy group at the University of Tasmania . 
@@26293295 <h> News <h> RACGP Environmental and Social Governance investment policy <p> As evidence of the planet 's changing environment continues to make itself clear , the RACGP is seeking member feedback to develop and finalise its Environmental and Social Governance investment policy . <p> The RACGP Environmental and Social Governance investment policy outlines how the college plans to invest with consideration for ethical , environmental , corporate governance and social issues . <p> With the World Health Organization declaring climate change ' the greatest threat to global health in the 21st century ' , issues of environmental health have become more relevant than ever for general practice . <p> However , Dr Tim Senior , a GP with a special interest in environmental issues , understands that the magnitude of the issue can make it hard for many healthcare practitioners to relate it in the context of day-to-day health . <p> ' The environment is such a big topic and often it 's talked about in terms of global impacts , changes in weather patterns , Antarctic ice sheets and things like that , ' Dr Senior told newsGP . <p> ' GPs are very involved in local communities @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that global perspective can often feel quite distant to us . ' <p> But Dr Senior believes GPs ' role in the community is exactly what makes them -- and the college -- well placed to perceive the ground-level effects of environmental health . <p> ' General practice is where the rubber hits the road , ' he said . ' You hear the talk of global impacts , but we 're dealing with the impacts on people in our own communities . ' <p> Professional responsibility The RACGP has a fiscal strategy to develop a diversified asset portfolio for the college 's long-term financial security . <p> ' As a high-profile , influential health organisation , the RACGP has a responsibility to demonstrate best standards and leadership in social and environmental sustainability , ' RACGP CEO Dr Zena Burgess told newsGP . <p> ' Anecdotal dialogue and formal feedback in recent years has increasingly demonstrated a desire among members , staff and other stakeholders to improve the RACGP 's sustainability practices . <p> ' The RACGP has made overt efforts in this respect , including establishment of the RACGP @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ also endeavoured to " go green " by transitioning member communications and official documentation , such as annual reports and meeting minutes , to digital formats wherever possible . ' <p> Implementation of the policy will begin immediately ; however , the RACGP is inviting member comment and feedback for further revision of this policy . <p> In 2015 , RACGP members raised a specific convocation item relating to investment in fossil fuels : <p> That the RACGP divests from all fossil fuel industry investments , including investments in financial institutions responsible for lending funding to other organisations for the purpose of fossil fuel exploration and extraction . <p> The item was carried and , in response , the RACGP Finance , Audit and Risk Management Committee developed an Environmental and Social Statement , considering : <p> emerging markets for the use of environmental services and environmentally-friendly products . <p> Practice matters GPs themselves can also have an important effect on the impacts of climate change by altering operations within their own practices . Dr Senior offered some practical suggestions for practices to try and reduce their own environmental impacts @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' he said . ' For example , things like insulation can make a big difference , switching to renewable energy providers , using solar either for hot water or for electricity . And then also looking at the waste and recycling in our practices and how well we 're doing that . <p> ' If people are really keen , there are mechanisms to have audits of how their practice is doing in terms of emissions and recycling . ' <p> ' One of the challenges is that it 's such a big problem , it 's difficult to know where to start , ' he said . <p> ' The effects in some other countries will be devastating , and that is one of the motivations of people who are really passionate about global health . <p> ' But the effects on our own community in Australia will also be really quite severe . There 's no one who 's left unaffected by this , which is why it should be everyone 's concern and everyone 's responsibility . '

@@26295567 <h> Taylor confirms no interest in emissions , but says he 's no climate skeptic <p> Taylor says his focus is only prices , not emissions , and while refusing to credit renewables for recent price falls , says there is a future for " commercially viable " hydro and solar . But makes no mention of wind energy . <p> New energy minister Angus Taylor has confirmed what was largely expected : that he has no interest in emission reductions under the remit handed him by prime minister Scott Morrison , and that his primary focus will be on reducing prices for consumers . <p> In his first public outing since his appointment on Sunday , after Morrison was elevated following a right-wing putsch against former prime minister Malcolm Turnbull . <p> Taylor , one of the key players in that putsch , and one of the Coalition 's harshest critics of renewable energy targets and emission reduction schemes , gave selected interviews to Murdoch publications on Thursday , along with some " media drops " to other newspapers ahead of his first speech as minister in Sydney on Thursday . <p> The interviews and speech confirm much @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ role -- which sees the energy and environment portfolios split , after having been united under Turnbull . <p> Taylor claims he is not a climate skeptic , but he dislikes emissions reduction schemes . He claims he is not anti-renewables , he just does n't like schemes that encourage them like the renewable energy target . <p> In reality , Taylor 's position on renewables does need some explaining , particularly given his appearance in 2013 along with Craig Kelly and Alan Jones at a " Wind Power Fraud Rally " organised by an anonymous website known for its violent threats , racism and mysogony . <p> It was interesting to note that when he talked about Australia 's energy resources in his speech on Thursday , there was no mention of wind : " It 's ironic that in a country with an abundance of natural resources -- coal , gas , water and solar -- we should be in this position , " he said of the country 's high energy prices . <p> He later mentioned both hydro ( his grandfather was chief engineer for Snowy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has some installed ) -- but again no mention of wind energy from the energy minister who has campaigned against many wind projects , including one near the family property . <p> As for his goal as minister , it is about prices , and nothing else . <p> " As the new Minister for Energy , my first and only priority is to reduce power prices , " he said in his speech . He said prices had already started the downward trend , but he credited this only to lower gas prices , and a focus on retail bills -- there was no credit for renewables . <p> This appears to ignore advice from most energy experts , including the Energy Security Council . <p> And new analysis released on Thursday suggests that the influx of renewables will cut wholesale prices by around half , with the huge quantities of wind and solar being brought on stream now and in the next two years . This is cutting prices in the spot market and the futures market . <p> This -- while contrary to Taylor 's view of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the original RET , through Tony Abbott 's Warburton Review , the Climate Change Authority research , the Finkel Review and the ESB modelling for the National Energy Guarantee -- that renewables will bring down prices , once they are built . <p> And that is the key . Now that they are being built , the analysis from Green Energy Markets shows , prices are falling as more than 7,200MW of wind and solar committed since 2016 are completed . <p> " We 've actually turned the corner on wholesale electricity prices and they 're now headed downward and will continue to decline substantially over the next few years , " GEM director Tristan Edis says . <p> Taylor , however , remains implacably opposed . He sees a future for " commercially viable " renewables -- but mentions only hydro and solar , as well as coal and gas . <p> He says will focus on recommendations from the Australian Competition and Consumer Commission ( whose chairman Rod Sims , like Taylor , is a fellow former director of consultancy group Port Jackson Partners . <p> These include @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the potential underwriting of " dispatchable " generation , and attempts to stop price gouging by the big " gen-tailers " . <p> Perhaps he could start with the government-owned gen-tailer , Snowy Hydro -- given the success of the Queensland government in bringing down prices . <p> He said while the ACCC did not recommend forced divestment of assets in bad cases , the government would consider it . <p> Taylor said that the flagged program to underwrite " new stable , low-cost generation for commercial and industrial customers " would be done " expeditiously " , but energy insiders , however , say that this is mostly talk . <p> There is a limit to what the government will be able to do before the next election because of the sheer complexity of what they are seeking , and because they need private contracts to exist before they can double up with their underwriting proposal . That wo n't stop them talking about it though . <p> As for the accusations of being a climate skeptic , Taylor says : " I am not sceptical about climate science @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ deeply sceptical of the economics of so many of the emissions reduction programs dreamed up by politicians , vested interests and technocrats around the world . " <p> That puts him firmly in the " do-nothing " camp championed by the likes of Bjorn Lomborg , the controversial Danish polemicist so admired by the Liberals when Tony Abbott was prime minister . <p> Another interesting aspect of Taylor 's speech was his claim that having a government commitment on emissions would not guarantee certainty -- as had been argued by Turnbull , Josh Frydenberg and most every utility and investor . <p> To some extent Taylor is right , no government can provide absolute certainty . That is not the nature of business . But who is going to invest on the basis that the government wo n't , over the long term , do anything about emissions . <p> And while this supposedly small government , low intervention administration proposes heavy intervention on markets and pricing , it refuses to price the one thing that absolutely must be priced , and that is carbon emissions . <p> The new @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and put a stop to some of the most shameless gaming of electricity markets , will not come into effect until 2021 -- some 6 years after it was first proposed , and resisted by the main energy utilities . <p> The regulator , despite some initial resistance , eventually agreed to the change , but said it would take four years to implement , and wo n't be seen until 2021 . <p> Giles Parkinson is a journalist of 30 years experience , a former Business Editor and Deputy Editor of the Financial Review , a columnist for The Bulletin magazine and The Australian , and the former editor of Climate Spectator .

@@26301328 <h> Australian Antarctic Division : Leading Australia 's Antarctic Program <h> Ancient ice quest harnesses heavy horsepower <p> Australia 's ground transport capability in Antarctica is set for a heavy-duty upgrade . <p> The Australian Antarctic Program has announced the first details of a new fleet of heavy tracked vehicles to support deep field operations , including the search for a million year ice core . <p> Project Lead , Anthony Hull , says the new traverse system will be able to meet Australia 's scientific and operational needs for the next two decades . <p> " This is a step-change in our ability to rapidly move large quantities of cargo and equipment deep inland in all weather conditions , and into areas that we ca n't traditionally access by aircraft , " Mr Hull said . <p> " It allows us to deploy scientists and support teams to some of the most remote and extreme parts of the Antarctic ice sheet for extended periods of time . " <p> The new system will have five heavy tractors and two snow groomers which will tow sled trains carrying food supplies , accommodation , communal areas , scientific facilities , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The traverse will be managed by a team of eight expeditioners and have the ability to reach up to 1500 kilometres inland . <p> " The first expedition is planned to depart Australia 's Casey research station in early 2021 and set up a mobile research station 1,200 kilometres inland at a location known as Dome C , " Mr Hull said . <p> " The station will be capable of supporting up to 16 people , flying in to undertake scientific research for up to three months . " <p> Australian Antarctic Division Glaciologist , Dr Tas van Ommen , said the new capability opens up the Antarctic interior to ambitious science projects , including the search for the Earth 's longest continuous ice core climate record . <p> " The million year ice core will be a window into a time when a major shift in the Earth 's climate system took place , and when the regular pacing ice ages gradually slowed , " Dr van Ommen said . <p> " We are working closely with our international collaborators to understand what caused this shift , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ day climate change . " <p> Scientists and engineers at the Australian Antarctic Division have also begun work on a new drill design for the project that is capable of extracting ice cores 3000 metres deep . <p> " Many of the components we need are specialist designs that have to be constructed and built in-house , and we have the right team to do that here in Hobart , " Dr van Ommen said . <p> " We are incorporating the latest technology that will allow us to recover the best quality core , to drill most efficiently , and hopefully get to the bottom of the ice sheet over a three-to-four year drilling period . @@26306644 <h> Australia 's new climate of fear <p> In 2005 , Peter Temple turned his back on the mean streets of Fitzroy to take his character Detective Joe Cashin down the Great Ocean Road . <p> With a blurred cover of a rocky coastline , Broken Shore announced its literary intentions and won a swag of international and national crime awards . But tight parliamentary numbers , uncertainty about the result of the coming Wentworth byelection and threatened fraying of the future parliamentary votes of at least two National party MPs contain ominous echoes reaching back 78 years in Australian political history . <h> World War 2 parallels <p> After an election in 1940 , the United Australia party ( precursor of the Liberal party ) led a minority Coalition government with the Country party ( now known as the National party ) . It was wartime , and the Japanese were threatening to occupy large swathes of south-east Asia @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ minister at the time , UAP leader Robert Menzies , spent four months in Britain in 1941 attending meetings of Winston Churchill 's war cabinet , and returned to a rancorous coalition . He was forced out of the UAP leadership soon after at a party meeting . Country Partly leader Arthur Fadden limped on for a few weeks as his replacement before two independents crossed the floor , and Labor leader John Curtin was asked by the governor-general , Lord Gowrie , to form a government . <p> Julie Bishop was a casualty of the leadership putsch , and has returned to the back bench . " <p> Parliamentary numbers are different now , and so of course are the times , but there are some powerful similarities between then and now ; a non-Labor government 's parlous parliamentary position , the potentially decisive influence of cross-benchers , and unrest continuing among Coalition ministers and MPs following a party-room @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Coalition ranks are mounting reports of abuse and intimidation , particularly against female Liberal MPs , during the days preceding the leadership putsch . @@26311611 <h> How A Battle To Build The Best Weather Model Impacts Everyone On Earth <p> Hurricane Sandy was one of the most destructive storms in US history . But beyond the catastrophic losses of life and property , the storm also dealt a blow to the American weather modelling community . The American weather model whiffed on the initial forecast , but its European counterpart was dead on . <p> Nearly six years later and the phrase " the Euro nailed Sandy " is still a running joke in the meteorological community . This phrase is shorthand for both the continued superiority of the European model 's ability to predict major weather events such as hurricanes , while also mocking people who obsess over a model that has its own deficiencies and occasional high profile whiffs . <p> Behind the scenes , though , the weather modelling arms race is heating up , with the American model getting a major upgrade early next year . <p> This competition about more than model supremacy : Lives and property are at stake . Witness last year , when weather disasters cost the US a record-setting $US306 billion ( $422 billion ) @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an estimated 3000 lives according to a government-commissioned survey published this week . <p> An increase in extreme weather brought on by climate change only makes it more important for our weather models to get it right . <p> Step outside and the weather is inherently obvious and easy to understand . The sun kisses your skin , the warm air causes the sweat to build behind your knees , maybe a slight breeze ruffles the grass . You pop your umbrella open as a thunderstorm pours and run to the basement as a tornado touches down . <p> But these basic experiences are the result of a chaotic series of interactions between the ocean , atmosphere , sea ice , incoming sunlight , and a host of other planetary-scale features . Models are how we try to make sense of all this complexity , and meteorologists use them as guidance when they 're forecasting hurricanes , snowstorms , or the chance of an afternoon shower . <p> There are a ton of weather models out there . National meteorological agencies make most of them but so do private companies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ company -- nailed Irma ? ) . <p> For much of the world , however , two models are the backbone of forecasting : The Euro , and the American model known as the Global Forecast System ( the GFS ) . These models are created and maintained by the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts ( ECMWF ) and the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration ( NOAA ) , respectively . <p> The two agencies were founded with divergent missions , which in part informs some of the differences in their weather models today . NOAA is focused on " climate , weather , oceans and coasts " while ECMMWF was established to " produce accurate climate data and medium-range forecasts " , or forecasts generally covering the range of three to seven days . <p> " The European Center has a very narrow mission compared to either the UK Met Office or the US National Weather Service ( which is part of NOAA ) , " Richard Rood , a weather and climate expert at the University of Michigan , told us . " That mission is medium @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ well . In the US , our resources -- which are quite substantial -- are diffused across a larger set of products . " <p> The " Euro nailed Sandy " meme arose because ECMWF had Sandy 's weird track pegged more than a week out . The GFS eventually worked out the same track for the storm . Similarly , the Euro bested the GFS when it came to 2015 's Hurricane Joaquin when it consistently and correctly predicted the storm would stay out to sea ( the GFS had it coming ashore along the Mid-Atlantic for a while ) . <p> John Morales , the chief meteorologist WTVJ in Miami who has seen no shortage of hurricane model runs , told us he relies heavily on both models for short-term forecasts . <p> " However , for days 3-7 , while still looking at the output from both models , I lean strongly towards the European global model , " he said . " It truly is difficult to beat , especially in the medium range . " <p> So what 's the Euro 's special sauce ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its disposal is measured in petaflops . To put that measure in perspective , it would take your measly human brain 31.7 million years to do the number of calculations that a one petaflop computing system can do in a second . NOAA has computers capable of reaching 8.4 petaflops , ECMWF tops out at around 8.5 petaflops . <p> All that power allows each group to run its model fast enough to spit out results for the entire planet a few times a day at fairly high resolution . The Euro recreates conditions up to 80km into the atmosphere and has a resolution on the ground of roughly 9km . The GFS runs simulates the atmosphere up to 53km above the surface with a resolution of 13km . <p> That resolution difference is partly why the GFS is n't as strong as its European counterpart when it comes to forecasting specific weather events . It 's essentially looking at the atmosphere with slightly blurry vision , while the Euro 's view is closer to 20/20 vision . <p> But there 's another key difference , which is how the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The Euro uses continuous data compiled from satellites and weather stations over a 12 hour period to set up initial conditions . These essentially give the model a sense of what the planet 's weather has been up to lately . <p> " This allows the Euro to make better use of observations and especially satellite observations which come in a nearly continuous stream , " Massimo Bonavita , a senior scientist working on at data assimilation at ECMWF , told Earther . <p> In comparison , the GFS assimilates a few snapshots of data . Basically , by the time the GFS gets out of the starting gates , the Euro already has a running start . <p> " The question is how do we do something disruptive to make the step to the next level , not how do we catch up ? " <p> But help is on the way on many fronts for the GFS . A new version of the model is coming in early 2019 . It will run on a new dynamic core , something that Brian Gross , a senior adviser for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Prediction ( which is part of NOAA ) , likened to the engine of a car . <p> " Replacing the engine is much like you do if you replace the engine in your car , " he said . " You will get a lot more miles out of this . That will future-proof us , allowing us to run higher resolution models with more advanced physics . " <p> The new engine is already being tested alongside the current GFS , and Gross said the early results are " pretty encouraging in terms of forecast track improvement and intensity improvements " for hurricanes . <p> The next generation of the GFS is hardly the end of the line for weather modelling . Neil Jacobs , a NOAA deputy administrator appointed by Trump , told Capital Weather Gang in April that improving weather modelling is a " top priority of the administration " . <p> There are also efforts afoot to create something called an unified forecast system , which will basically take all the models under NOAA 's purview from the GFS to ice , ocean and land @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ model game of telephone . <p> " You do n't exactly have one model that does everything , " Rood , who is part of task force working on that project , said . " What the unified system gives you is a more controlled environment for prediction problems . " <p> Those problems can range from whether it will rain in Omaha in the next few hours to whether Nebraska will face drought conditions in the next four weeks .
